A proof of Dolbeault geometric Langlands for GL 2 \operatorname{GL}_{2} with reduced spectral curves
Yukinobu Toda

Abstract
. In our previous paper with Tudor Pădurariu, we introduced the notion of limit categories for moduli stacks of Higgs bundles and formulated the Dolbeault geometric Langlands correspondence. These limit categories are expected to provide an effective “classical limit” of the categories of D-modules on the moduli stack of bundles, and our formulation links categorical Donaldson–Thomas theory with the geometric Langlands correspondence. In this paper, we prove the above Dolbeault geometric Langlands correspondence for GL 2 \operatorname{GL}_{2} over the locus in the Hitchin base where the spectral curves are reduced. This is the first non-trivial case in which the relevant moduli stacks are not quasi-compact, and the use of limit categories is essential to the formulation and proof of the correspondence. Our approach also outlines a strategy for proving the correspondence in greater generality and explains the current obstructions to such an extension.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main result

Let
C
be a smooth projective curve of genus
g
, and let
G
be a reductive group with Langlands
dual
{}^{L}G
.
Let

\displaystyle h\colon\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\to\mathrm{B}

be the derived moduli stack of
G
-Higgs bundles on
C
, with Hitchin fibration
h
.
For each connected component
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
with
\chi\in\pi_{1}(G)
,
we have the quasi-compact open substack of semistable Higgs bundles

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi).

However, the stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
of
all
Higgs bundles is not quasi-compact unless
G
is a torus.

In our previous paper with Tudor Pădurariu
[
34
]
, we introduced a dg-category (called a

limit category
)

(1.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}\subset\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}

for
\chi\in\pi_{1}(G)
and
w\in Z_{G}^{\vee}
, where
Z_{G}
is the center of
G
and

\mathcal{N}
denotes the nilpotent singular support condition
[
5
]
.
The category (
1.1
) is expected to provide an effective “classical limit” of the categories of

\mathrm{D}
-modules on the moduli stack of
G
-bundles, and it overcomes several subtleties arising from
(quasi- or ind-)coherent sheaves on stacks that are not quasi-compact.

We then proposed a version of the
Dolbeault geometric Langlands conjecture

(with nilpotent singular support), which may be regarded as
a classical limit of the
geometric Langlands correspondence, as follows:

Conjecture 1.1
.

(
[
34
]
)

There is a
\mathrm{B}
-linear equivalence

(1.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}

which is compatible with Wilson/Hecke operators.

So far, for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
, Conjecture
1.1
is only known over the elliptic locus

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}\subset\mathrm{B}
by
[
6
]
, where the spectral curves are irreducible.
Over the elliptic locus, we have

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}=\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}=\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}.

Here
(-)^{\mathrm{st}}
stands for the stable part. In particular,
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
is
quasi-compact over the elliptic locus, indeed it is “almost” a smooth symplectic variety, up to the trivial derived structure
k[-1]
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe.

Let

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset\mathrm{B}

be the open subset corresponding to reduced (but not necessarily irreducible) spectral curves.
The condition (
1.3
) essentially fails in this case;
there are strictly semistable Higgs bundles in the left-hand side of (
1.2
), and the stack

\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
is not quasi-compact in the right-hand side of (
1.2
)
because of infinitely many Harder–Narasimhan strata.
In this paper, we prove the following result:

Theorem 1.2
.

(Theorem
5.1
)

For
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
, Conjecture
1.1
holds over the locus
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset\mathrm{B}
.

The result of Theorem
1.2
is the first non-trivial case beyond the elliptic locus in which the condition (
1.3
) essentially fails. In particular, the relevant moduli stacks are not quasi-compact, yet the framework still admits Hecke operators. The result of Theorem
1.2
and its proof show that the notion of limit category from
[
34
]
is essential both for the formulation
and for the proof.

Remark 1.3
.

(1) The equivalence (
1.1
) is also expected to be compatible with parabolic induction functors, and hence with the semiorthogonal decompositions into quasi-BPS categories constructed in
[
34
]
. One can also verify this compatibility in the setting of Theorem
1.2
, but we omit the proof since it is not needed for the proof of Theorem
1.2
. However, compatibility with parabolic induction is expected to be necessary beyond
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
; see Subsection
1.5
.

(2) In
[
34
]
, we also proposed a version without nilpotent singular support:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{IndCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{IndL}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w},

which may be regarded as a classical limit of the renormalized geometric Langlands correspondence in
[
14
, Remark 1.6.9]
.
Our approach in this paper, together with compatibility with parabolic induction, should also prove (
1.4
) over
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}
for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
.
Details may appear elsewhere.

1.2.
Summary of the ingredients of Theorem
1.2

An outline of the proof of Theorem
1.2
will be given in Subsection
1.4
.
Here we summarize the main ingredients:

(i) Limit categories on non-quasi-compact Higgs stacks.

Beyond the elliptic locus, the stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
is not quasi-compact,
due to the presence of infinitely many Harder–Narasimhan strata.
The categories of (quasi- or ind-)coherent sheaves on such a stack are
not well-behaved in general,
whereas the limit category introduced in
[
34
]
is well-behaved (e.g. compactly generated) and admits Hecke operators,
making it the correct framework beyond the elliptic locus.

(ii) A left adjoint to the Hitchin section.

Using the formalism of limit categories, we construct a left adjoint

s_{!}
to
s^{*}
for the Hitchin section
s\colon\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}
.
The object
s_{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B}}
plays the role of the “classical limit” of the vacuum Poincaré sheaf in the geometric Langlands correspondence
[
14
]
,
and it is crucial for the Whittaker normalization.

(iii) Fourier–Mukai transform via the (extended) Arinkin sheaf.

We use Arinkin’s Cohen–Macaulay extension of the Poincaré bundle
[
6
,
27
]
(and its extension to the
generically regular locus) to construct the Fourier–Mukai functor

\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}
from the semistable locus to the generically regular locus.

(iv) Wilson/Hecke compatibility for
\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}
.

We prove that
\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}
intertwines Wilson and Hecke operators.
The argument uses an explicit description of the Arinkin sheaf via Haiman’s isospectral Hilbert scheme
[
19
]
.

(v) Generation/conservativity and Whittaker normalization on
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
.

We exploit compact generation by iterated Wilson operators and, over the locus of reduced spectral curves,
the conservativity of right adjoints of Hecke operators; together with a proof of the Whittaker
normalization for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
, this yields Theorem
1.2
.

1.3.
History

The Dolbeault geometric Langlands conjecture was proposed by Donagi–Pantev
[
12
]

as a classical limit of the geometric Langlands correspondence. Recall that the geometric Langlands correspondence is an equivalence

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{LocSys}_{{}^{L}G})\simeq\text{D-mod}(\mathrm{Bun}_{G})

proposed in
[
7
,
5
]
and proved in
[
14
,
3
,
10
,
4
,
15
]
.
In its original form, the Dolbeault geometric Langlands conjecture is stated as an equivalence,
see
[
8
, Conjecture 1.3]
and also
[
26
]
for a motivation from physics

(1.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G})\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}).

Note that we have two Hitchin fibrations over the common base
\mathrm{B}
:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{L}h}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}.}

It was proved in
[
12
]
that these fibrations are dual abelian fibrations over the locus

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{sm}}\subset\mathrm{B}
consisting of smooth spectral curves.
Consequently, the equivalence (
1.5
) over
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{sm}}
follows immediately from the classical
Fourier–Mukai transform
[
31
]
.
Arinkin
[
6
]
further extended this to the elliptic locus

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}\subset\mathrm{B}
.

However, beyond the elliptic locus
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}\subset\mathrm{B}
, essentially nothing is known about an
equivalence of the form (
1.5
).
Indeed, outside
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}
the stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}
is not quasi-compact, and the dg-categories of
quasi-coherent (or ind-coherent) sheaves on it are not well-behaved; for instance, they may fail to be compactly generated.
At present, it seems unrealistic to prove (
1.5
) beyond
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}
within this naive framework.
By contrast, it is proved in
[
34
]
that the dg-category (
1.1
) is compactly generated,
even though
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
is not quasi-compact.

The limit category (
1.1
) was introduced in
[
34
]
precisely to circumvent these categorical
subtleties when working with (quasi- or ind-)coherent sheaves on non-quasi-compact stacks.
For
g\geqslant 2
, we have open immersions

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{sm}}\subsetneq\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}\subsetneq\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}\subsetneq\mathrm{B}.

Theorem
1.2
gives the first instance in which the Dolbeault geometric Langlands correspondence
is proved beyond
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}
, in a framework that is appropriate for non-quasi-compact stacks.

We also remark that Melo–Rapagnetta–Viviani
[
30
]
, building on ideas of Arinkin
[
6
]
, proved a derived equivalence for the compactified Jacobians of reduced planar curves; see Theorem
2.19
. In their setting, one considers compactified Jacobians for which semistability coincides with stability, and hence their result does not cover the statement of Theorem
1.2
. Moreover, the derived category of compactified Jacobians does not admit Hecke operators. Nevertheless, we use the result of
[
30
]
in the final step of the proof of Theorem
1.2
, namely in establishing the Whittaker normalization.

Our formulation in Conjecture
1.1
also reveals a strong connection between categorical
Donaldson–Thomas theory
[
40
]
and the geometric Langlands correspondence;
see
[
34
, Section 1]
for further discussion.
Moreover, the limit category (
1.1
) admits Hecke operators, and this turns out to be essential
in the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Also see
[
34
, Section 1.4]
for the relation to
topological mirror symmetry by Hausel–Thaddeus
[
23
,
24
]
.

1.4.
Outline of the proof of Theorem
1.2

Here we outline the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
We first point out that the statement is trivial for
g\leqslant 1
;
for
g=0
we have
\mathrm{B}^{\rm{red}}=\emptyset
and for

g=1
the reduced spectral curve
\mathcal{C}_{b}

is a disjoint union of the elliptic curve
C
, so the classical
Fourier-Mukai transform
[
31
]
applies. In what follows,
we assume that
g\geqslant 2
.
In Step 1 to Step 5 below, we assume
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
with
r\geqslant 2
.

Step 1
.

Construction of the functor.

We first construct a functor

(1.10)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{greg}})_{w^{\prime}},

where
\chi^{\prime}=\chi+(r^{2}-r)(g-1)
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}

is the open substack of generically regular Higgs bundles.
The functor (
1.10
) is defined as a Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel given by
Arinkin’s Cohen–Macaulay extension of the Poincaré line bundle on the locus of regular Higgs bundles;
see
[
6
,
27
]
.

Step 2
.

Compatibility with Wilson/Hecke operators.

We next show that the functor (
1.10
) is compatible with Wilson/Hecke operators, in the following sense.

Theorem 1.4
.

(Theorem
3.3
)

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{W}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}).}

Here
S=\mathrm{Tot}(\Omega_{C})
is a (non-compact) Calabi–Yau surface.
The functor
\mathrm{W}
is the
Wilson operator
, defined via the universal Higgs bundle as a correspondence.
The functor
\mathrm{H}
is the
Hecke operator
, defined via the stack of Hecke correspondences.
The proof of Theorem
1.4
is based on an explicit construction of the Arinkin sheaf using
Haiman’s isospectral Hilbert scheme
[
19
]
.

Step 3
.

Classical limit of the vacuum Poincaré sheaf.

In this step we essentially use the limit category.
Let
s
be the Hitchin section

\displaystyle s\colon\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}

defined by the universal spectral curve.
The map
s
is not proper and its image is not closed outside the semistable locus; nevertheless, the theory of limit categories allows us to construct
a
left adjoint
to
s^{*}
,

\displaystyle s_{!}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{B})\to\operatorname{L}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})=\operatorname{IndL}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})^{\mathrm{cp}},

where
(-)^{\mathrm{cp}}
denotes the full subcategory of compact objects.
The existence of
s_{!}
is one of the key features of the limit category; such a left adjoint to
s^{*}
does not exist
in the setting of (quasi- or ind-)coherent sheaves.

Step 4
.

Whittaker normalization.

We expect that, under the conjectural equivalence of Conjecture
1.1
,
the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}}
corresponds to

s_{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B}}
(up to a shift of weights). We refer to this expected identification as the

Whittaker normalization
.

We now restrict to the locus of reduced spectral curves. Fix an open subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red,cl}},

and write
\mathcal{H}=\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathcal{B}
, etc.
Then
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}=\mathcal{H}
(since any Higgs bundle with reduced spectral curve is generically regular), so the functor (
1.10
) induces a functor

(1.15)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}(w))_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(\chi))_{w^{\prime}}.

The following conjecture formulates the Whittaker normalization over reduced spectral curves:

Conjecture 1.5
.

(Conjecture
4.4
)

There is an isomorphism in
\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})
.

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}})\cong s_{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}.

Step 5
.

Equivalence under the Whittaker normalization.

Fix
c\in C
.
By applying the Wilson operator to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}}

d
times (with
r\mid(d+\chi)
)
and then taking the right adjoint, we construct a functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}^{R}_{d,c}\colon\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(S^{\times d}).

We show that the objects obtained by iterated Wilson transforms of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}}

compactly generate
\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})
; this implies that
\mathrm{W}^{R}_{d,c}
is conservative for
d\gg 0
.

Similarly, by applying the Hecke operator to
s_{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}

d
times and taking the right adjoint,
we construct a functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{R}_{d,c}\colon\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(\chi))_{w^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(S^{\times d}).

We also prove the conservativity of
\mathrm{H}^{R}_{d,c}
under an additional assumption
(Assumption
4.18
) on the singularities of the spectral curves corresponding to points of
\mathcal{B}
,
which is automatically satisfied for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
.

Then Theorem
1.4
, together with Conjecture
1.5
and the compact generation statement above,
yields the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{W}^{R}_{d,c}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\operatorname{QCoh}(S^{\times d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(\chi))_{w^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{H}^{R}_{d,c}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{R}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{QCoh}(S^{\times d}).}

Here
\Phi^{R}
denotes the right adjoint of
\Phi
, which exists formally by the adjoint functor theorem.

Combining this diagram with the conservativity of the right adjoints of the Wilson and Hecke operators, we obtain:

Theorem 1.6
.

(Theorem
4.29
)

Assume Conjecture
1.5
. Then the functor (
1.15
) induces a fully faithful functor

(1.20)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(\chi))_{w^{\prime}}.

Moreover, under Assumption
4.18
(which is satisfied for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
), the functor (
1.20
) is an equivalence.

Step 6
.

Proof of the Whittaker normalization for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
.

Since both sides of (
1.20
) are unchanged (up to equivalence) under the weight shift

(\chi,w)\mapsto(\chi^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, in the case
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
it remains to prove Conjecture
1.5
.
More precisely, we prove:

Theorem 1.7
.

(Proposition
5.12
)

Conjecture
1.5
holds for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
.

We prove this by constructing an explicit resolution of the Arinkin sheaf by vector bundles and evaluating their weights.
Along the way, we also show that the functor
\Phi
preserves the quasi-BPS categories considered in
[
34
,
32
]
. It reduces the Whittaker normalization to an analogous problem for a perturbed stability condition for which semistability coincides with stability, after passing to an
étale cover of the Hitchin base. Then we use the result of
[
30
]
to deduce Theorem
1.7
.
At present, the argument of Theorem
1.7
relies on special features of the case
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
.

1.5.
Toward a proof of Conjecture
1.1

Our proof in this paper suggests a strategy for a complete proof of
Conjecture
1.1
in the case
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
.

(1) First, construct a functor

(1.21)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}\bigl(\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}\bigr)_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{IndCoh}\bigl(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)\bigr)_{w^{\prime}}

given by an extension of the Arinkin sheaf.

(2) Show that
\Phi
and its right adjoint
\Phi^{R}
are compatible with
Wilson/Hecke operators, as well as with the parabolic induction functors
considered in
[
34
]
.

(3) Prove the Whittaker normalization

\Phi\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}}\bigr)\cong s_{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B}}.

(4) Show that the dg-category

\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}\bigl(\mathrm{Higgs}_{\operatorname{GL}_{r}}(w)\bigr)_{-\chi^{\prime}}

is compactly generated by iterated applications of Wilson operators to

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}}
, together with the essential images of
the parabolic induction functors.

(5) Steps (1)–(4), together with an induction on
r
, imply that the functor

\Phi
in fact gives a fully faithful functor

(1.22)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}\bigl(\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}\bigr)_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}\bigl(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)\bigr)_{w^{\prime}}.

(6) Show that the dg-category

\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}\bigl(\mathrm{Higgs}_{\operatorname{GL}_{r}}(\chi)\bigr)_{w^{\prime}}

is compactly generated by iterated applications of Hecke operators to

s_{!}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{B}}
, together with the essential images of the
parabolic induction functors. This would imply that the fully faithful functor
(
1.22
) is essentially surjective, hence an equivalence.

Step (1) may be the most difficult. In the case
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
and
L
-twisted Higgs
bundles, a Cohen–Macaulay extension of the Arinkin sheaf is constructed
in
[
27
]
; however, this remains open in other cases. Note that in
[
14
]

the geometric Langlands functor is constructed by producing an action of

\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{LocSys}_{{}^{L}G})
on
\text{D-mod}(\mathrm{Bun}_{G})
via the
geometric Satake correspondence. There has been recent progress on the classical limit of the geometric Satake correspondence
[
11
,
28
]
; however, at this moment it seems extremely hard to use it to produce
an action of
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{{}^{L}G}^{\mathrm{ss}})
on

\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})
.

Over the locus
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
, steps (1), (2), (4) and (5) are already
established for any
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
, and in that setting one does not need to include parabolic
induction in (4). The remaining obstructions are to prove (3) and (6), which we
established for
G=\operatorname{GL}_{2}
in this paper.

]

1.7.
Notation and Conventions

Conventions on stacks

We work over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
.
Unless stated otherwise, all (derived) stacks are locally Noetherian and quasi-separated over
k
.
For a stack
\mathcal{X}
over
k
, we write
\mathcal{X}(k)
for the set of
k
-valued points of
\mathcal{X}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cl}}
its classical truncation.
A stack is called
QCA
if it is quasi-compact with affine geometric stabilizer groups.

For a scheme
Y
with an action of an algebraic group
G
, we write
Y/G
for the associated quotient stack.
We also use the notion of good moduli spaces of stacks, see
[
2
]
(and also
[
1
]
for derived stacks).

For a morphism of derived stacks
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, let
\mathbb{L}_{f}
and
\mathbb{T}_{f}
denote the
f
-relative cotangent and tangent complexes. The morphism
f
is called
quasi-smooth
if
\mathbb{L}_{f}
is perfect of cohomological amplitude
[-1,1]
.
When
\mathcal{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}k
, we write
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{L}_{f}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{T}_{f}
.
A derived stack
\mathcal{X}
over
k
is

quasi-smooth
if
\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}k
is quasi-smooth.
For
m\geq 0
, its
m
-shifted cotangent stack is

\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}[m]:=\operatorname{Spec}_{\mathcal{X}}\,\mathrm{Sym}\big(\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{X}}[-m]\big)\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}.

For a finite-dimensional
k
-vector space
V
and
m\geq 0
, set

V[-m]:=\operatorname{Spec}\mathrm{Sym}\big(V^{\vee}[m]\big),

where
\mathrm{Sym}\big(V^{\vee}[m]\big)
has zero differentials.

All the fiber products are derived fiber products, and we use the notation
\square
for the
Cartesian square. We also use the following
reduced fiber product
:
For a morphism of stacks

\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Y}
, its
reduced fiber product
is defined to be

(\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{Y})^{\mathrm{cl,red}}
, and use the notation
\square^{\mathrm{red}}
for the
reduced Cartesian square.

1.7.1.
Notations for (ind)coherent sheaves

For a dg-category
\mathcal{C}
, denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{cp}}\subset\mathcal{C}
the subcategory of compact objects. The dg-category
\mathcal{C}
is called
compactly generated
if
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{cp}})
, where
\operatorname{Ind}(-)
is the ind-completion.
A dg-functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}
is called
continuous

if it commutes with taking direct sums.

We use the notation of
[
16
]
for (ind)coherent sheaves.
For a derived stack
\mathcal{X}
, we denote by

\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})
the dg-category of coherent sheaves,
by
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X})
the dg-category of quasi-coherent sheaves,
and by
\operatorname{IndCoh}(\mathcal{X})
the dg-category of ind-coherent sheaves.
The heart of a standard t-structure on
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})
is denoted by

\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{X})
.
For a closed substack
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{X}
, we denote by
\operatorname{QCoh}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{X})

the subcategory of
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X})
consisting of objects
A
such that

A|_{\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{Z}}=0
.
We have the
local cohomology functor

(1.23)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathcal{Z}}(-)\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X})\to\operatorname{QCoh}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{X})

which gives a right adjoint of the inclusion
\operatorname{QCoh}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{X})\subset\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X})
.

For derived stacks
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
such that either one of them is QCA, there is an equivalence,
see
[
13
, Corollary 4.3.4]
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X})\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}).

We use the notation
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{Y})
for the subcategory of the left
hand side, which corresponds to
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})

under the above equivalence.
For
\mathcal{E}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})
and
A\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{Y})
,
we often write
\mathcal{E}\boxtimes A
for
\mathcal{E}\otimes p_{\mathcal{Y}}^{*}A

where
p_{\mathcal{Y}}
is the projection to
\mathcal{Y}
.
If
\mathcal{X}
is quasi-smooth, then
there is also a duality equivalence:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathrm{op}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X}),\ (-)\mapsto\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}).

1.7.2.
Notations for
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-weights

For a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe
\mathcal{X}\to X
, there is an orthogonal weight
decomposition

\displaystyle\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})=\bigoplus_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})_{w},

where
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})_{w}
is the subcategory of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-weights
w
.
For the Brauer class
\beta
corresponding to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe
\mathcal{X}\to X
,
we can identify
\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{X})_{w}
with the dg-category of
\beta^{w}
-twisted coherent sheaves

\operatorname{Coh}(X,\beta^{w})
, see
[
29
, Section 2.1.3]
.

For an object
A\in\operatorname{Coh}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
,
let
A=\oplus_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{w}

be its weight decomposition. We write
A^{>0}=\oplus_{w>0}A_{w}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{wt}(A)\subset\mathbb{Z}
the set of
w\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
A_{w}\neq 0
,
and
\mathrm{wt}^{\mathrm{max}}(A)\in\mathbb{Z}
is the maximal element in
\mathrm{wt}(A)
.
We will use the following map

\displaystyle c_{1}\colon K(B\mathbb{G}_{m})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to H^{2}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{Q},

which is given by
c_{1}(A)=\sum_{w}w\cdot\dim A_{w}
.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we recall Higgs bundles, their moduli stacks, limit categories,
and the formulation of Dolbeault geometric Langlands correspondence in
[
34
]
.
We also recall Arinkin’s construction of Cohen–Macaulay extension of Poincaré line bundle,
and the associated Fourier-Mukai functor.

2.1.
Moduli stacks of Higgs bundles

Let
C
be a smooth projective curve over
k
of genus
g\geqslant 2
. For
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
, a
G
-Higgs bundle

consists of

(2.1)

\displaystyle(F,\theta),\ \theta\colon F\to F\otimes\Omega_{C}

where
F\to C
is a rank
r
vector bundle.
We denote by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}=\coprod_{\chi\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)

the derived moduli stack of
G
-Higgs bundles,
and each
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
is the connected component
corresponding to (
2.1
) such that
\deg F=\chi
.
It is a zero-shifted symplectic stack
[
38
]
; indeed we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}=\Omega_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}

where
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is the moduli stack of
G
-bundles.

A Higgs bundle (
2.1
) is called
regular
at
p\in C
if

\theta|_{p}\colon F|_{p}\to F|_{p}\otimes\Omega_{C}|_{p}\cong F|_{p}
is a regular
matrix. It is
regular
(resp.
generically regular
) if
\theta|_{p}
is regular for any

p\in C
(resp. regular at the generic point
p\in C
).
We denote by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{greg}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)

the open substack of regular Higgs bundles, generically regular Higgs bundles, respectively.

We have the (derived) Hitchin map

\displaystyle h\colon\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\to\mathrm{B}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Gamma(\Omega_{C}^{i})=\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}\times k[-1].

We have the Cartesian square, see
[
7
, 2.2.4]
for example

\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{cl}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}.}

Each point
b\in\mathrm{B}
corresponds to the
spectral curve

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{b}\subset S:=\mathrm{Tot}_{C}(\Omega_{C}).

By the spectral construction, the Hitchin fiber

h^{-1}(0)
corresponds to rank one torsion-free
sheaves on
\mathcal{C}_{b}
.
Under the above correspondence, regular Higgs bundles correspond to
line bundles on
\mathcal{C}_{b}
, generically regular Higgs bundles correspond
to rank one torsion-free sheaves on
\mathcal{C}_{b}
which are line bundle on the generic
point of
\mathcal{C}_{b}
, respectively.

We denote by
\mathrm{C}\to\mathrm{B}

the universal spectral curve.
We have the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{S\times\mathrm{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\times\mathrm{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B.}

Here the left horizontal arrow is a closed immersion, and the other arrows are
projections.
We have the universal Higgs bundle

(2.10)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{F},\vartheta),\ \mathrm{F}\to C\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G},\ \vartheta\colon\mathrm{F}\to\mathrm{F}\boxtimes\Omega_{C}.

Here
\mathrm{F}
is a rank
r
vector bundle and
\vartheta
is the universal
Higgs field. By the spectral construction, it gives a universal sheaf

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\in\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{C}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})

such that
\pi_{*}\mathrm{E}\cong\mathrm{F}
. By abuse of notation,
we also write
\mathrm{E}
for its push-forward to
S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}
.

From the diagram (
2.1
), the stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}
is almost classical; there is only
a factor of
k[-1]
. By the following lemma, it actually splits:

Lemma 2.1
.

There is an equivalence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{cl}}\times k[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}.

Proof.

The moduli stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\simeq\Omega_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathrm{Sym}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}).

Let
\pi^{\mathrm{rig}}
be the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-rigidification

\displaystyle\pi^{\mathrm{rig}}\colon\mathrm{Bun}_{G}\to\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{\mathrm{rig}},

which is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe. We have an exact triangle

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\pi^{\mathrm{rig}}}\to\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\to(\pi^{\mathrm{rig}})^{*}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{\mathrm{rig}}}.

We show that the above sequence (
2.12
) splits.
The first map of the above sequence is the natural map

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}[1]\to p_{\mathrm{Bun}*}\mathcal{H}om(\mathrm{F},\mathrm{F})[1]

where
\mathrm{F}\to C\times\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is the universal vector bundle
and
p_{\mathrm{Bun}}\colon C\times\mathrm{Bun}_{G}\to\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is the projection.
The natural map

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{C\times\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathrm{F},\mathrm{F})

is a split injection, where the splitting is given by
(-)\to\mathrm{tr}(-)/r
.
Therefore we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}om(\mathrm{F},\mathrm{F})\cong\mathcal{O}_{C\times\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\oplus\mathcal{H}om(\mathrm{F},\mathrm{F})_{0}

where the second factor is the traceless part.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}

\displaystyle\cong p_{\mathrm{Bun}*}\mathcal{O}_{C\times\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}[1]\oplus p_{\mathrm{Bun}*}\mathcal{H}om(\mathrm{F},\mathrm{F})_{0}[1]

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}[1]\oplus(H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}})\oplus p_{\mathrm{Bun}*}\mathcal{H}om(\mathrm{F},\mathrm{F})_{0}[1],

which implies that the sequence (
2.12
) splits.

Then we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathrm{Sym}((\pi^{\mathrm{rig}})^{*}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{\mathrm{rig}}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}[1])\simeq\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{cl}}\times k[-1].

∎

2.2.
Moduli stacks of semistable Higgs bundles

A
\operatorname{GL}_{r}
-Higgs bundle
(F,\theta)
is called
(semi)stable
if
for any sub-Higgs bundle
(F^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\subset(F,\theta)

we have

\displaystyle\frac{\deg F^{\prime}}{\operatorname{rank}F^{\prime}}<(\leqslant)\frac{\deg F}{\operatorname{rank}F}.

We denote by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{st}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)

the open substack of (semi)stable Higgs bundles.
It is an Artin stack of finite type, in particular it is
quasi-compact.

Remark 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{b}\subset S
be the spectral curve of
(F,\theta)
,
and
E\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{C}_{b})
the corresponding rank one torsion-free sheaf. Then
(F,\theta)
is (semi)stable if and only if
E
is Gieseker (semi)stable with respect to the polarization
\pi^{*}h
where
\pi\colon\mathcal{C}_{b}\to C
is the projection and
h
is an ample divisor on
C
, see
[
25
]
for the notion of Gieseker (semi)stability.
Below, (semi)stable sheaf on
\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{C}_{b})
means Gieseker
(semi)stable sheaf with respect to
\pi^{*}h
.

We have the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{st}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{st}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}}

where the vertical arrows are good moduli space morphisms.
The derived scheme
\mathrm{H}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}

is projective over
\mathrm{B}
, the horizontal arrows are open
immersions,
and the left vertical arrow is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe.
Moreover the classical truncation of
\mathrm{H}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{st}}

is a smooth symplectic variety.
If
(r,\chi)
is coprime, then
we have
\mathrm{H}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{st}}=\mathrm{H}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}
, but
in general the inclusion is strict.

2.3.
Open locus of Hitchin base

There are derived open subschemes

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{sm}}\subsetneq\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}\subsetneq\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}\subsetneq\mathrm{B}

where
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
(resp.
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}},\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{sm}}
) corresponds to reduced (resp. irreducible, smooth) spectral curves.
The stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
is quasi-compact over
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell}}
, but over

\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
it is not quasi-compact.

Example 2.3
.

Let
G=\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and
g=2
.
For
b\in\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
,
suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{b}
is a reducible
nodal curve

\mathcal{C}_{b}=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
, where
C_{1}\cap C_{2}
consists of
two points
x_{1},x_{2}
.
The stack

h^{-1}(b)
contains an open substack

\displaystyle h^{-1}(b)^{\mathrm{reg}}=\coprod_{l_{1}+l_{2}=\chi+2}\mathcal{P}^{l_{1},l_{2}}

where
\mathcal{P}^{l_{1},l_{2}}
consists of line bundles
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{C}_{b}
such that
l_{i}=\deg\mathcal{L}|_{C_{i}}
. It is easy to see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{l_{1},l_{2}}\cong\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{1}}(C_{1})\times\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{2}}(C_{2})\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}.

Here
\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{i}}(C_{i})
is the moduli space of degree
l_{i}
-line bundles on
C_{i}
,
which is a
g
-dimensional abelian variety since
C_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}C
.

We have the following diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{1}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{b}.}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{2}}

Here
\nu
is the normalization of
\mathcal{C}_{b}
and each
\nu_{i}
is a partial normalization at
x_{i}
.
We denote by

\mathcal{P}_{1}^{l_{1},l_{2}},\ \mathcal{P}_{2}^{l_{1},l_{2}},\ \mathcal{P}_{12}^{l_{1},l_{2}}

the moduli stacks of line bundles on
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,1},\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b,2},\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{b}
respectively,
whose restrictions to
C_{i}
have degree
l_{i}
.
We have isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i}^{l_{1},l_{2}}\cong\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{1}}(C_{1})\times\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{2}}(C_{2})\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{12}^{l_{1},l_{2}}\cong\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{1}}(C_{1})\times\mathrm{Pic}^{l_{2}}(C_{2})\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}.

Then the closure of
\mathcal{P}^{l_{1},l_{2}}
in
h^{-1}(b)
is described as (cf.
[
42
, Figure 1]
)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{l_{1},l_{2}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{l_{1},l_{2}}\cup\nu_{1*}\mathcal{P}_{1}^{l_{1},l_{2}-1}\cup\nu_{1*}\mathcal{P}_{1}^{l_{1}-1,l_{2}}\cup\nu_{2*}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{l_{1},l_{2}-1}\cup\nu_{2*}\mathcal{P}_{2}^{l_{1}-1,l_{2}}

\displaystyle\cup\nu_{*}\mathcal{P}_{12}^{l_{1}-2,l_{2}}\cup\nu_{*}\mathcal{P}_{12}^{l_{1}-1,l_{2}-1}\cup\nu_{*}\mathcal{P}_{12}^{l_{1},l_{2}-2}.

The above situation is visualized as follows (here
\leadsto
stands for the degeneration)

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{1,-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}^{0,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{1}^{1,-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{2}^{1,-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0,-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{2}^{0,-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{1}^{-1,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{2}^{-1,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{12}^{1,-3}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{12}^{0,-2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{12}^{-1,-1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{12}^{-2,0}}

From the above picture,
h^{-1}(b)
is obtained by gluing the closures of

\mathcal{P}^{l_{1},l_{2}}
, and it is connected. Because there are infinitely many pairs
(l_{1},l_{2})

for a given
\chi
, the stack
h^{-1}(b)
is not quasi-compact.

2.4.
Limit categories

Here we recall limit categories defined in
[
34
]
, which itself is inspired and based on
the works
[
39
,
20
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a quasi-smooth derived stack which is of finite presentation over
k
, and
its cotangent complex is self-dual

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{M}}\simeq\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}.

For
x\in\mathfrak{M}(k)
,
we set

\displaystyle T_{x}:=\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}|_{x}),\ \mathfrak{g}_{x}:=\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{M}}|_{x}).

Note that
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is the Lie algebra of

G_{x}:=\mathrm{Aut}(x)
.

Given a map

\displaystyle\nu\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathfrak{M},\ \mathrm{pt}\mapsto x

we have the corresponding cocharacter

\mathbb{G}_{m}\to G_{x}
.
We let
\mathbb{G}_{m}
act on
\mathfrak{g}_{x},T_{x}

through the above cocharacter, and regard them as elements of
K(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
.
The map
\nu
may not be quasi-smooth, but it extends to a quasi-smooth map
(unique up to 2-isomorphisms)

(2.18)

\displaystyle\nu^{\mathrm{reg}}\colon\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\vee}[-1]/\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathfrak{M}

called
regularization
, see
[
21
, Construction 3.2.2]
,

[
34
, Construction-Definition 3.1]
.
It satisfies that the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{M}}|_{x}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\vee}[-1]/\mathbb{G}_{m}}|_{0}=\mathfrak{g}_{x}[1]

induces an isomorphism on
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(-)
.
We also denote by
\iota
the natural
morphism

\displaystyle\iota\colon\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\vee}[-1]/\mathbb{G}_{m}\to B\mathbb{G}_{m}.

Let
\delta\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathfrak{M})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Recall the notations about
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-weights in Subsection
1.7.2
.

Definition 2.4
.

(
[
34
, Definition 3.3]
)
When
\mathfrak{M}
is QCA, the subcategory

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}\subset\operatorname{Coh}(\mathfrak{M})

is defined to consist of objects
\mathcal{E}

such that, for any map

\nu\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathfrak{M}

with
\nu(0)=x
and regularization (
2.18
),
we have

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathrm{wt}(\iota_{\ast}\nu^{\mathrm{reg}\ast}(\mathcal{E}))\subset\left[\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(T_{x}^{<0}),\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(T_{x}^{>0})\right]+\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})+c_{1}(\nu^{*}\delta).

Remark 2.5
.

The regularization (
2.18
) is needed since the naive pull-back
\nu^{*}\mathcal{E}
is not necessarily
bounded and
\mathrm{wt}(\nu^{*}\mathcal{E})
is not necessarily a finite set.

On the other hand if

\mathcal{E}
is perfect then
\nu^{*}\mathcal{E}
is bounded and the above issue is avoided.
In this case, the condition (
2.20
) can be simplified as

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{wt}(\nu^{*}\mathcal{E})\subset\left[\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(\nu^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{<0}),\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(\nu^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{>0})\right]+c_{1}(\nu^{\ast}\delta).

The proof is similar to
[
34
, Lemma 3.7]
, and we omit it.

For non-quasi-compact case, the limit category is defined as follows:

Definition 2.6
.

(
[
34
, Section 3.7]
)
For a general (not necessarily quasi-compact) quasi-smooth derived stack

\mathfrak{M}
satisfying (
2.17
), we define

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndL}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}:=\lim_{\mathcal{U}\subset\mathfrak{M}}\operatorname{Ind}(\operatorname{L}(\mathcal{U})_{\delta})

where the limit is over all the QCA open substacks
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathfrak{M}
, and define

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}\subset\operatorname{IndL}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}

to be the subcategory of compact objects.
For a smooth stack
\mathcal{X}
, we
call
\operatorname{L}(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}})_{\delta}
the
limit category
.

Let
\Omega_{\mathfrak{M}}[-1]
be the
(-1)
-shifted cotangent of
\mathfrak{M}
, and
let

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\subset\Omega_{\mathfrak{M}}[-1]

be the nilpotent cone; a fiber of
\Omega_{\mathfrak{M}}[-1]\to\mathfrak{M}

at
x\in\mathfrak{M}(k)
is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
of
\operatorname{Aut}(x)
by the condition (
2.17
),
and the fiber of
\mathcal{N}\to\mathfrak{M}
at
x
consists of nilpotent elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
.
We also have the subcategories with nilpotent singular supports
[
5
]
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}\subset\operatorname{L}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta},\ \operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}\subset\operatorname{IndL}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}.

Remark 2.7
.

Instead of compact objects, we can also consider

\displaystyle\widetilde{\operatorname{L}}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}:=\lim_{\mathcal{U}\subset\mathfrak{M}}\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{U})_{\delta},\ \widetilde{\operatorname{L}}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}:=\lim_{\mathcal{U}\subset\mathfrak{M}}\operatorname{L}(\mathcal{U})_{\delta}.

They contain
\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}
,

\operatorname{L}(\mathfrak{M})_{\delta}
respectively, but in general
they are strictly larger, see
[
34
, Remark 3.18]
.

2.5.
Dolbeault geometric Langlands conjecture

We recall the formulation of Dolbeault geometric Langlands conjecture
proposed in
[
34
]
, specialized to the case of
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
and with
nilpotent singular supports.

For
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})=\bigoplus_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{IndCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})_{w}

where each summand corresponds to objects with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-weight
w

for the diagonal torus

\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset\operatorname{Aut}(F,\theta)
.
We have the subcategory

(2.22)

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}\subset\operatorname{IndCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}

by setting
\delta
to be

\displaystyle\delta=\det(\mathrm{F}_{c})^{\otimes w/r}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Here
\mathrm{F}_{c}
is the rank
r
-vector bundle

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}_{c}:=\mathrm{F}|_{c\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}}\to\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}

for a fixed
c\in C
and
\mathrm{F}
is the universal bundle (
2.10
).
The subcategory (
2.22
) is independent of a choice of
c\in C
.

Theorem 2.8
.

(
[
34
, Theorem 1.15]
)

The dg-category (
2.22
) is compactly generated by compact objects

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}\subset\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}.

Remark 2.9
.

In
[
34
, Theorem 1.15, Theorem 7.18]
, the compact generation is stated
for limit categories without nilpotent singular support condition. However using
[
34
, Theorem 7.17]
, the same argument of loc. cit. applies to show
the compact generation with nilpotent singular supports.

The following is a version of Dolbeault geometric Langlands conjecture in
[
34
]
,
specialized to
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
and with nilpotent singular supports:

Conjecture 2.10
.

(
[
34
, Conjecture 1.1]
)

For
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
and
(\chi,w)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there is a
\mathrm{B}
-linear equivalence

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi}\simeq\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}.

Remark 2.11
.

By Theorem
2.8
and the compact generation of ind-coherent sheaves
for QCA stacks
[
13
]
(also see
[
5
, Corollary 9.2.7]
in the case of nilpotent singular supports and global complete intersection type, which is applied to
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}
), Conjecture
2.10
is equivalent to an equivalence
between compact objects

\displaystyle\operatorname{Coh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi}\simeq\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}.

Remark 2.12
.

There exist equivalences

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w+r}

by taking
\otimes\det\mathrm{F}_{c}
. Also there is an equivalence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi+r),\ (F,\theta)\mapsto(F,\theta)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(c)

which preserves the semistability. Therefore we can regard
(\chi,w)

as an element of
(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z})^{2}
.

For a quasi-smooth derived scheme
\mathcal{B}
with
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}}\simeq\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
and a map
\mathcal{B}\to\mathrm{B}
(e.g.
\mathcal{B}
is
an open subscheme in
\mathrm{B}
or
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}
),
we simply write

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}:=\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathcal{B}.

We also write
\mathcal{H}(\chi)
,
\mathcal{H}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}
in a similar way.
We say that

DL holds over
\mathcal{B}
if there is a
\mathcal{B}
-linear equivalence

(2.24)

\displaystyle\operatorname{IndCoh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi}\simeq\operatorname{IndL}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{H}(\chi))_{w}.

Remark 2.13
.

For an open subscheme
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{B}
, suppose that
the DL holds over
\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}\subset\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}
. Then DL also
holds over
\mathcal{B}
.
Indeed an equivalence (
2.24
) for
\mathcal{B}
follows from
that for
\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}
by taking

\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(k[-1])
by Lemma
2.1
and the diagram (
2.1
).

2.6.
Quasi-BPS categories

By applying the definition of limit category to the
quasi-compact moduli stack
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}
, we obtain
the
quasi-BPS category
(with nilpotent singular support)

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{w}\subset\operatorname{Coh}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{w}.

The quasi-BPS category is related to categorification of BPS invariants in
Donaldson-Thomas theory, see
[
32
,
35
,
34
]
.

We have the open immersion

\displaystyle j\colon\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}}\subset\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)

which induces the pull-back functor

(2.25)

\displaystyle j^{*}\colon\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}\to\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{w}.

The following is one of the key properties of limit categories:

Theorem 2.14
.

(
[
34
, Theorem 7.19]
)

The functor (
2.25
) admits a fully-faithful left adjoint

\displaystyle j_{!}\colon\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{w}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}.

Remark 2.15
.

From the proof of
[
34
, Theorem 7.18, 7.19]
, the image of
j_{!}

is described as follows: for any map
\nu\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)

corresponding to a
Harder-Narasimhan filtration
, the
weight condition (
2.20
) is strict on the left.
Such a map
\nu
is of the form

(2.26)

\displaystyle\nu\colon\mathrm{pt}\mapsto\bigoplus_{i=1}^{a}E_{i},\ \frac{\deg E_{i}}{\operatorname{rank}E_{i}}>\cdots>\frac{\deg E_{a}}{\operatorname{rank}E_{a}}

where each
E_{i}
is semistable, and
with
\mathbb{G}_{m}
weight
\mu_{i}
on
E_{i}

satisfying
\mu_{1}>\cdots>\mu_{a}
.

In particular for
a perfect complex
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathfrak{M}=\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi)
,
it is in the image of
j_{!}
if it satisfies the
following stronger condition than (
2.21
)
for any map
\nu\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
as in (
2.26
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{wt}(\nu^{*}\mathcal{E})\subset\left(\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(\nu^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{<0}),\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(\nu^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{>0})\right]+c_{1}(\nu^{\ast}\delta).

2.7.
Arinkin sheaf

Let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}
be an open subset, and define
\mathcal{H}
as in (
2.23
).
Consider

\displaystyle(\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H})^{\sharp}:=(\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{reg}})\cup(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}).

Then there is a line bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}\to(\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H})^{\sharp}

whose fiber at
(A_{1},A_{2})
for
A_{i}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{C}_{b})

for
b\in\mathcal{B}
is

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}|_{(A_{1},A_{2})}=\det\chi(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})\otimes\det\chi(A_{1})^{-1}\otimes\det\chi(A_{2})^{-1}\otimes\det\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}_{b}}).

The above formula makes sense since either
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
is a line bundle on
\mathcal{C}_{b}
,
so that
\chi(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})
makes sense.

Formally let
\mathcal{C}:=\mathrm{C}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathcal{B}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}:=\mathrm{E}|_{\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}).

Then on
\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}=\det p_{13*}(p_{12}^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes p_{23}^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes(\det p_{13*}p_{12}^{*}\mathcal{E})^{-1}\otimes(\det p_{13*}p_{23}^{*}\mathcal{E})^{-1}\otimes\det p_{13*}\mathcal{O}.

Here
p_{ij}
are the projections from
\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}

onto the corresponding factors.

Arinkin
[
6
]
constructed the maximal Cohen–Macaulay
extension of
\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}
to
\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}

when
\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell,cl}}
. Outside the locus
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell,cl}}
, i.e. when
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{ell,cl}}\subsetneq\mathcal{B}
, it is observed in
[
27
]
that
\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}
can be extended
to the generically regular Higgs locus, namely on

\displaystyle(\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H})^{\flat}:=(\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\cup(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H})

Theorem 2.16
.

(
[
6
,
27
]
)

The line bundle
\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}
extends to a maximal
Cohen–Macaulay sheaf

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\flat}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}((\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H})^{\flat}).

Moreover it is flat over both sides of
\mathcal{H}
.

We use the symbol
\mathcal{P}
to denote its restriction to

\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}
;

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}^{\flat}|_{\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}).

2.8.
Fourier-Mukai functor via Arinkin sheaf

For
b\in\mathcal{B}
and
E\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{C}_{b})
, we have

\deg\pi_{*}E=w
if and only if

\displaystyle\deg_{\mathcal{C}_{b}}(E)

\displaystyle:=\chi(E)-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}_{b}})

\displaystyle=w+(r^{2}-r)(g-1)(=:w^{\prime}).

Here
\deg_{\mathcal{C}_{b}}
is the degree as a sheaf on
\mathcal{C}_{b}
.
The Arinkin sheaf (
2.29
) on

\mathcal{H}(w)\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}(\chi)
has bi-weight

(\chi^{\prime},w^{\prime})
by the description (
2.27
),
therefore it induces the
\mathcal{B}
-linear functor

(2.30)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathcal{H}(\chi)^{\mathrm{greg}})_{w^{\prime}},

defined by the Fourier-Mukai functor

\displaystyle\Phi(-)=p_{2*}(p_{1}^{*}(-)\otimes\mathcal{P}).

The functor (
2.30
) restricts to the functor

(2.31)

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(\chi)^{\mathrm{greg}})_{w^{\prime}}

since
\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}
admits a good moduli space which is projective over
\mathcal{B}

and
\mathcal{P}
is flat over
\mathcal{H}
.

Remark 2.17
.

If
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red,cl}}
, then

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}
since any rank one torsion-free sheaf on a reduced curve is generically a line bundle. Therefore we have the maximal
Cohen–Macaulay extension
\mathcal{P}

on
\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}
.
In particular in this case, the Arinkin sheaf induces the functor

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(\chi))_{w^{\prime}}.

Lemma 2.18
.

The functor
\Phi
in
(
2.30
) fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(\chi)^{\mathrm{greg}})_{w^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(w+r)^{\mathrm{ss}})_{-\chi^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}(\chi)^{\mathrm{greg}})_{w^{\prime}+r}}

Here the left vertical arrow is induced by
the isomorphism
\mathcal{H}(w)^{\mathrm{ss}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathcal{H}(w+r)^{\mathrm{ss}}

given by
\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(c)
for
c\in C
and the right vertical arrow is
given by
\det\mathcal{F}_{c}
, where
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{F}|_{C\times\mathcal{H}}
,
see Remark
2.12
.

Proof.

The commutativity of (
2.18
) easily follows by comparing the kernel objects
of both compositions on
(\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H})^{\sharp}
, using the formula
for
\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}
, and the uniqueness of Cohen–Macaulay extension.
∎

By applying the construction (
2.29
) for
\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}((\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}})^{\mathrm{cl}}).

By Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}\simeq(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}})^{\mathrm{cl}}\times k[-1].

We define

(2.36)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}:=\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{k[-1]}\in\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}).

Note that
\mathrm{P}
is not in the heart of the standard t-structure.
We have the
\mathrm{B}
-relative Fourier-Mukai transform

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}),\ (-)\mapsto p_{2*}(p_{1}^{*}(-)\otimes\mathrm{P}).

Here
p_{i}
are the projections onto the corresponding factors.
Similarly to the above, it restricts to the functor

(2.37)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}).

2.9.
Fourier-Mukai transform of compactified Jacobians of reduced curves

Let
X
be a reduced projective curve with at worst planar singularities
(e.g.
X
is the spectral curve
X=\mathcal{C}_{b}
for
b\in\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{red}}
).
Here we recall Fourier-Mukai transform of compactified Jacobians of
X

via Arinkin sheaf, proved in
[
30
]
. Below we use the notation in
[
30
, Section 1.1]
.

Let
\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the irreducible components of
X
. A
polarization
is a tuple of
rational numbers
\underline{q}=\{\underline{q}_{C_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
for each
C_{i}\subset X

such that
\lvert\underline{q}\rvert:=\sum_{i\in I}q_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
A torsion-free rank one sheaf
I\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(X)
of Euler characteristic
\chi(I)=\lvert\underline{q}\rvert

is called
\underline{q}
-(semi)stable

if for any subcurve
Y\subsetneq X
we have

\displaystyle\chi(I_{Y})(\geqslant)>\sum_{C_{i}\subset Y}\underline{q}_{C_{i}}.

A polarization
\underline{q}
is called
general
if there are no strictly
\underline{q}
-semistable sheaves.
A
fine compactified Jacobian
of
X
is the fine moduli space
\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q})

of torsion-free rank one sheaves of degree
\lvert\underline{q}\rvert
which are
\underline{q}
-semistable (or equivalently
\underline{q}
-stable) with respect to a general polarization
\underline{q}
on
X
.

A fine compactified Jacobian is a good moduli space of the moduli stack of
rank one semistable sheaves
\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q})
.
The good moduli space morphism

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q})\to\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q})

is a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe. Indeed it is a trivial
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbe
because of the existence of universal sheaf on
X\times\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q})
,
see
[
30
, Section 2.1]
.

The construction of the Arinkin sheaf in the previous subsection yields the
Cohen–Macaulay sheaf

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{J}}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q})\times\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q}^{\prime}))

of bi-weight
(\lvert\underline{q}^{\prime}\rvert+p_{a}-1,\lvert\underline{q}\rvert+p_{a}-1)
,
where
p_{a}
is the arithmetic genus of
X
.

Theorem 2.19
.

(
[
30
, Theorem A]
)

The Fourier-Mukai functor

(2.38)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathcal{J}}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q}))_{-\lvert\underline{q}^{\prime}\rvert-p_{a}+1}\to\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q}^{\prime}))_{\lvert\underline{q}\rvert+p_{a}-1}.

with kernel
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{J}}
is an equivalence. Moreover the inverse of

\Phi_{\mathcal{J}}
is given by the kernel object

\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{J}})[p_{a}]
.

Remark 2.20
.

In
[
30
, Theorem A]
, the result is stated for
\lvert\underline{q}\rvert=\lvert\underline{q}^{\prime}\rvert=1-p_{a}
; in this case the functor (
2.38
) is canonically identified
with

\displaystyle\Phi_{J}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q}^{\prime})).

In other cases, the functor (
2.38
) is canonically identified with

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Phi_{J}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q}),\beta^{-\lvert\underline{q}^{\prime}\rvert-p_{a}+1})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q}^{\prime}),\beta^{{}^{\prime}\lvert\underline{q}\rvert+p_{a}-1}).

Here
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
are the Brauer classes corresponding to
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-gerbes

\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q}),\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{X}(\underline{q}^{\prime})

respectively. Since
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
are trivial classes, both sides in (
2.39
)
the above functor is
uncanonically
identified with

\displaystyle\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q}))\to\operatorname{Coh}(\overline{J}_{X}(\underline{q}^{\prime})).

The source of the uncanonicity is a choice of trivializations of
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
;
as stated just below of
[
30
, Theorem A]
, the above non-canonical functor
is still an equivalence, which implies that Theorem
2.19
holds without
the condition
\lvert\underline{q}\rvert=\lvert\underline{q}^{\prime}\rvert=1-p_{a}
.

Remark 2.21
.

The notion of
\underline{q}
-stability includes Gieseker semistable sheaves.
Indeed for a
\mathbb{Q}
-ample divisor
H
on
X
, and
E\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(X)
,
let
\underline{q}_{C_{i}}=c\cdot\deg(H|_{C_{i}})
, where
c\in\mathbb{Q}
is determined by

c\cdot\sum_{i\in I}\deg(H|_{C_{i}})=\chi(E)
.
Then
E
is
H
-Gieseker (semi)stable if and only if it is
\underline{q}
-(semi)stable.

In particular if we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{X}(\chi)^{H\text{-ss}}
the moduli stack of
H
-semistable
sheaves
E
on
X
with degree
\chi
, the result of Theorem
2.19

yields an equivalence for generic
H
(i.e. when
H
-semistable equals to
H
-stable)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{M}_{X}(\chi)^{H\text{-ss}})_{-w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{M}_{X}(w)^{H\text{-ss}})_{\chi}.

3.
Compatibility of Wilson/Hecke operators

In this section, we prove the compatibility of Wilson/Hecke operators under
the Fourier-Mukai functor given by the Arinkin sheaf. Throughout this section,

C
is a smooth projective curve of genus
g\geqslant 2
over

k
, and
G=\operatorname{GL}_{r}
.

3.1.
Wilson operator

Recall from (
2.11
) that we have the universal sheaf

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}\in\operatorname{Coh}(S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}).

It is a perfect complex, and
is a push-forward by the closed immersion

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}\times_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\hookrightarrow S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}.

The
(left) Wilson operator
is given by the functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})

defined by

\displaystyle(-)\mapsto p_{\mathrm{H}*}((-)\boxtimes\mathrm{E})

where
p_{\mathrm{H}}\colon S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\to\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}
is the projection. Note that
p_{\mathrm{H}}
is not proper, but
the functor
\mathrm{W}
preserves coherent sheaves
since
\mathrm{E}
is supported on (
3.1
)
which is proper over
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}
.

3.2.
Hecke operator

Now we consider Hecke operators.
Let
\mathrm{Hecke}_{G}
be the derived moduli stack
classifying Higgs bundles
(F_{i},\theta_{i})
for
i=1,2
with
injections

(3.2)

\displaystyle(F_{1},\theta_{1})\subset(F_{2},\theta_{2})

such that
F_{2}/F_{1}
is isomorphic to a skyscraper sheaf of a closed point in
C
.
By the spectral construction, it is equivalent to the derived
moduli stack which classifies
exact sequences in
\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(S)

(3.3)

\displaystyle 0\to E_{1}\to E_{2}\to\mathcal{O}_{x}\to 0

where
E_{i}
are compactly supported pure one-dimensional sheaves on
S
and
x\in S
.

We have the evaluation morphisms

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hecke}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{3}^{\sharp},\mathrm{ev}_{2}^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}(1)\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}.}

Here

\mathfrak{M}(1)
is the derived moduli stack which classifies skyscraper sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{x}
for

x\in S
, which is explicitly described as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}(1)\simeq S\times k[-1]\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}.

The map
\mathrm{ev}_{i}^{\sharp}
for
i=1,2
sends (
3.3
) to
E_{i}

and
\mathrm{ev}_{3}^{\sharp}
sends it to
\mathcal{O}_{x}
.

We modify the stack
\mathrm{Hecke}_{G}
and define the following:

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hecke}^{\prime}_{G}:=\mathrm{Hecke}_{G}\times_{\mathfrak{M}(1)}(S\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}).

It admits the following evaluation maps

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hecke}^{\prime}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}.}

Here
\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{2}
are induced from
\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{\sharp},\mathrm{ev}_{2}^{\sharp}
and

\mathrm{ev}_{3}
is the composition of the projection to
S\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}

with the projection onto
S
.
We have the following lemma, whose proof will be given in Subsection
6.1
:

Lemma 3.1
.

The maps
\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{2}
are quasi-smooth
and proper of relative virtual dimension
1
.
The maps
(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2}),(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{1})
are also quasi-smooth and proper of relative virtual dimension

-1
.

The
(left) Hecke operator

is the functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G})

defined by

\displaystyle(-)\mapsto\mathrm{ev}_{1*}(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})^{*}(-).

Note that the functor
\mathrm{H}
preserves coherent sheaves by
Lemma
3.1
. Moreover we have the following lemma, which is not used in this section but will be relevant
in the next section.
It is essentially proved in
[
34
, Proposition 9.8]
, but the statement in loc. cit.
is different, so we will give its proof in Subsection
6.2
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{H}
restricts to the functor of limit categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{L}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi))_{w}\to\operatorname{L}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}(\chi-1))_{w}.

There is an open substack

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hecke}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}\subset\mathrm{Hecke}_{G}

consisting of (
3.2
) such that either (equivalently, both) of the
\theta_{i}
is/are generically regular.
Then we have the following Cartesian squares

\textstyle{S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hecke}^{\prime\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\times\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{Hecke}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}.}

Therefore we also have the left Hecke operator on the generically regular
locus

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}})

by
\mathrm{H}=\mathrm{ev}_{1*}(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})^{*}
in the upper diagram of (
3.2
).

3.3.
Wilson/Hecke compatibility

Recall the functor (
2.37
) constructed by the Arinkin sheaf.
The purpose of this section is to prove the following compatibility with
Wilson/Hecke operators:

Theorem 3.3
.

The following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}^{\mathrm{ss}}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{W}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\otimes\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\mathrm{P}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{greg}}).}

We reduce Theorem
3.3
to the statement for classical stacks.
We fix an open subset
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{cl}}
and use the notation
as in Subsection
2.5
, especially the notation for the
stack
\mathcal{H}
in (
2.23
). Note that
\mathcal{H}
is classical by the diagram (
2.1
).
We have the Wilson operator over
\mathcal{B}

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})

defined to be

\displaystyle(-)\mapsto p_{\mathcal{H}*}((-)\boxtimes\mathcal{E}),\ \mathcal{E}:=\mathrm{E}|_{S\times\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(S\times\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}).

Here
p_{\mathcal{H}}
is the projection

S\times\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}\to\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}}
.

The Hecke operator over
\mathcal{B}
is given as follows.
By taking the base change of the diagram (
3.2
) with respect to

\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{B}
, we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}ecke^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\times\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

Here (by abuse of notation) we have used the same notation
\mathrm{ev}_{i}
when pulled back via
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{B}
.
We have the Hecke operator over
\mathcal{B}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})

defined to be

\displaystyle(-)\mapsto\mathrm{ev}_{1*}(\mathrm{ev}_{3},\mathrm{ev}_{2})^{*}(-).

Recall the Fourier-Mukai functor
\Phi
as in (
2.31
), defined
by the Arinkin sheaf:

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{reg}}).

We have the following lemma:

Lemma 3.4
.

The commutativity of (
3.3
) follows if the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{W}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\otimes\Phi}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(S)\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}).}

Proof.

The lemma follows since tensor product of the diagram (
3.4
) with
\operatorname{Coh}(k[-1])
gives the diagram (
3.3
).
∎

3.4.
Dual operators

We will reduce the commutativity of the diagram (
3.4
) to a commutativity of
a similar diagram for
dual
Wilson/Hecke operators.

The
dual Wilson operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}^{\vee}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(S)

is defined to be

\displaystyle(-)\mapsto(-)\boxtimes\mathcal{E}.

The
dual Hecke operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{\vee}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(S)

is defined to be

\displaystyle(-)\mapsto(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{3})_{*}\mathrm{ev}_{2}^{*}(-).

The above functor is associated with the following
diagram (compare with (
3.3
))

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}ecke^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{3})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}\times S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}.}

As in Lemma
3.1
, both of the maps
\mathrm{ev}_{2}
and
(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{3})
are quasi-smooth and proper.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that the diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{W}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{H}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\otimes\operatorname{Coh}(S)}

commutes. Then the diagram (
3.4
) commutes.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that the
kernel objects of the clockwise (resp. anticlockwise) compositions of the diagrams
(
3.4
) and (
3.5
) are the same.

Alternatively, for
A\in\operatorname{Coh}(S)
let

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{A}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ss}})

be the corresponding Wilson operator. It is recovered from the dual
Wilson operator

\mathrm{W}^{\vee}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{A}(-)=p_{\mathcal{H}*}(\mathrm{W}^{\vee}(-)\boxtimes A).

Similarly the corresponding Hecke functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{A}\colon\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})\to\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}})

is recovered from the dual Hecke operator
\mathrm{H}^{\vee}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{A}(-)=p_{\mathcal{H}*}(\mathrm{H}^{\vee}(-)\boxtimes A).

Therefore the lemma holds.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The commutativity of the diagram (
3.5
) over the elliptic locus
is proved in
[
18
, Theorem 6.3.6]
. However the argument in loc. cit. does not apply beyond the elliptic locus, even for the reduced locus,
since it essentially uses the fact that
\Phi
is an equivalence over the elliptic locus. Instead we prove the commutativity of (
3.5
) by using the explicit construction of the Arinkin sheaf.

3.5.
Hecke eigensheaf property of the Arinkin sheaf

By Lemma
3.5
, the result of Theorem
3.3
is reduced to showing the commutativity
of (
3.5
). We first show the commutativity of (
3.5
)
for points over
\mathrm{Higgs}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}
.

For a Higgs bundle
(F,\theta)
, let
E\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(S)

be the corresponding sheaf. Let

(3.39)

\displaystyle\tau_{E}\colon\operatorname{Spec}k\to\mathcal{H}

be the corresponding map. Then using the Arinkin sheaf (
2.28
), we have

(3.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{E}:=(\tau_{E}\times\operatorname{id})^{*}\mathcal{P}^{\flat}\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{H}_{b}^{\mathrm{greg}})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Coh}^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}).

Here
b\in\mathcal{B}
corresponds to the spectral curve
\mathcal{C}_{b}
of
E
,

\tau_{E}\times\operatorname{id}
is the map

\displaystyle\tau_{E}\times\operatorname{id}\colon\operatorname{Spec}k\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}=\mathcal{H}_{b}^{\mathrm{greg}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{greg}}

and
\mathcal{H}_{b}:=b\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{H}
.

The sheaf (
3.40
) is constructed in
[
6
,
27
]
explicitly using Haiman’s isospectral
Hilbert schemes, which we recall now.
Recall that the Hilbert scheme of points

\operatorname{Hilb}^{d}(S)

parametrizes zero-dimensional closed subschemes
Z\subset S
with length
d
.
The
isospectral Hilbert scheme
